---
# Nguyễn Phú Trọng, Vietnam, 2023

## 1. Overview

Nguyễn Phú Trọng was General Secretary of the Communist Party of Vietnam (CPV), serving his third term. In Vietnam’s party-state system, this was the country’s most influential office: the Party controls senior appointments, strategic policy, the legislature, security institutions, and the state apparatus. The president, prime minister, National Assembly chair, and other Politburo members remained important institutional actors; implementation depended on the government, ministries, provincial authorities, police, military, and courts.

Trọng’s influence was therefore extensive but collective rather than fully personal. He set the political line, chaired the anti-corruption campaign, shaped senior personnel decisions, and represented Vietnam in major strategic diplomacy. His major 2023 priorities were party discipline, anti-corruption, political stability, economic recovery, national security, and “bamboo diplomacy.” The government maintained peace and macroeconomic stability, expanded ties with the United States, and continued rapid development, but growth fell below target and political freedoms remained severely restricted.

Information quality was uneven. Official Vietnamese sources provide detailed evidence of formal decisions and government objectives but omit or sanitize dissent, coercion, corruption networks, and policy failures. Independent reporting and international human-rights organizations provide important counterevidence, but access to victims, defendants, journalists, officials, and local records is restricted.

## 2. Evidence records

### Record 1 — Trọng’s formal office and practical authority

**Fact.** Nguyễn Phú Trọng was General Secretary of the CPV Central Committee throughout 2023 and the dominant figure in Vietnam’s party-state system. The General Secretary was not the head of government in the constitutional sense, but the Party controlled the state’s strategic direction and senior personnel. His authority was mediated by the Politburo, Central Committee, prime minister, president, National Assembly, military, police, and provincial party structures.

**Why it matters.** Attribution of national outcomes to Trọng should focus on decisions and campaigns directed through the Party, while avoiding treating every administrative outcome as his personal act.

**Strongest source.** “The Passing of Vietnam Party Chief Nguyen Phu Trong,” Center for Strategic and International Studies, 2024. [Direct link](https://www.csis.org/analysis/passing-vietnam-party-chief-nguyen-phu-trong)

**Locator.** Discussion of the General Secretary as the most important of Vietnam’s “four pillars,” alongside the prime minister, president, and National Assembly chair; discussion of collective leadership and institutional checks.

**Period status.** Period-wide background, applicable to 2023.

**Attribution strength.** High for strategic Party direction and personnel politics; moderate for individual administrative implementation.

**Complicating evidence.** The same source emphasizes that Vietnam remained a system of collective leadership, so Trọng’s role should not be equated with unconstrained personal rule.

**Cautions.** The source is a retrospective analytical essay rather than a contemporaneous constitutional or official record.

**Independent support.** [Freedom House, Vietnam 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/vietnam/freedom-world/2023); [Le Monde retrospective analysis](https://www.lemonde.fr/en/obituaries/article/2024/07/20/nguyen-phu-trong-symbol-of-vietnamese-authoritarianism-dies-in-hanoi_6691391_15.html).

---

### Record 2 — 2023 development goals and below-target economic performance

**Fact.** Vietnam’s official 2023 plan targeted approximately 6.5% GDP growth, macroeconomic stability, inflation control, infrastructure, social protection, health insurance coverage, poverty reduction, and stronger anti-corruption enforcement. Vietnam’s National Statistics Office later reported 5.05% year-on-year GDP growth in 2023.

**Why it matters.** The government preserved stability and growth, but failed to meet its principal growth target amid weak global demand, export pressure, domestic financial problems, and real-estate stress.

**Strongest source.** “Kế hoạch phát triển kinh tế xã hội năm 2023,” Government of Vietnam, 30 November 2022. [Direct link](https://baochinhphu.vn/nghi-quyet-ve-ke-hoach-phat-trien-kinh-te-xa-hoi-nam-2023-102221130171714827.htm)

**Locator.** Official plan: target of 6.5% growth and goals for inflation, poverty, health insurance, employment, infrastructure, welfare, security, and anti-corruption, paragraphs displayed under the headings on GDP targets and principal tasks.

**Period status.** Target adopted before the period; outcome measured during 2023.

**Attribution strength.** Moderate to high for the strategic program; lower for the final growth result, which depended on the government, State Bank, ministries, global conditions, and firms.

**Complicating evidence.** The government’s midterm review claimed macroeconomic stability and recovery despite international headwinds. [Midterm Central Committee speech](https://baochinhphu.vn/phat-bieu-cua-tong-bi-thu-nguyen-phu-trong-be-mac-hoi-nghi-trung-uong-giua-nhiem-ky-10223051717175679.htm)

**Cautions.** Official statistics and official self-assessment may understate distributional hardship or implementation failures. Nominal GDP indicators should not be interpreted as real improvements in living standards without additional evidence.

**Independent support.** [Vietnam National Statistics Office, 2023 annual report](https://www.gso.gov.vn/en/data-and-statistics/2024/02/socio-economic-situation-in-the-fourth-quarter-and-2023/); [World Bank, Vietnam economic update](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/08/10/vietnam-s-economic-growth-slows-due-to-global-headwinds-and-internal-constraints).

---

### Record 3 — Expansion of anti-corruption enforcement and elite turnover

**Fact.** Trọng continued the “blazing furnace” anti-corruption campaign in 2023. The campaign contributed to the resignation of President Nguyễn Xuân Phúc in January, after the Party attributed responsibility to him for wrongdoing by officials under his authority. Several senior officials and ministers were investigated, removed, or disciplined.

**Why it matters.** This was one of Trọng’s clearest personal political programs. It demonstrated the Party’s capacity to punish senior officials, but also generated uncertainty, administrative caution, and questions about selective enforcement and factional competition.

**Strongest source.** “Vietnam legislature approves president’s resignation amid graft crackdown,” Reuters, 18 January 2023, reproduced by Euronews. [Direct link](https://www.euronews.com/2023/01/19/vietnam-politics)

**Locator.** Reported parliamentary approval of Phúc’s resignation, the Party’s attribution of responsibility for officials’ violations, and the context of the anti-graft campaign.

**Period status.** Event during 2023; campaign inherited from earlier years.

**Attribution strength.** High for the campaign’s strategic direction; moderate for individual investigations and prosecutions.

**Complicating evidence.** Enforcement can improve deterrence and accountability, but political analysts and investors also warned that it could slow decisions, intensify factional conflict, and create risk-averse administration.

**Cautions.** Official explanations for personnel removals are often formulaic and do not disclose the full evidence or political context. Reuters reporting relied partly on official statements and anonymous sources.

**Independent support.** [International IDEA, Vietnam January 2023](https://www.idea.int/democracytracker/report/vietnam/january-2023); [Reuters reporting reproduced by MarketScreener](https://www.marketscreener.com/news/latest/Vietnam-president-quits-blamed-for-ministers-violations-42748640/); [U.S. Department of State 2023 human-rights report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107615.html).

---

### Record 4 — Political repression and constrained civil liberties

**Fact.** Vietnam continued to restrict opposition politics, independent journalism, online speech, civil society, peaceful assembly, and environmental activism. Activists and journalists were prosecuted under national-security or tax provisions. In September 2023, environmental activist Hoàng Thị Minh Hồng was sentenced to three years in prison for tax evasion.

**Why it matters.** The government’s political stability was achieved within a system that did not permit meaningful electoral alternation or independent scrutiny. Attribution to Trọng is strongest for the Party’s overall security and ideological line, not necessarily for each prosecution.

**Strongest source.** “Vietnam: Freedom in the World 2023 Country Report,” Freedom House. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/vietnam/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator.** Sections describing restrictions under Decree 72 and the Cybersecurity Law, imprisonment of journalists and activists, and barriers to political participation.

**Period status.** The report mainly covers 2022 and early 2023; it is a near-period baseline. The Hồng prosecution occurred in 2023.

**Attribution strength.** High for the system-wide policy environment; moderate for individual cases.

**Complicating evidence.** The government characterizes prosecutions as lawful enforcement against tax violations, national-security offenses, or abuses of democratic freedoms. These explanations should be recorded but assessed against the procedural independence of courts and access to case files.

**Cautions.** International rights organizations depend on interviews and secondary information because access is restricted. Official and independent accounts are sharply polarized.

**Independent support.** [U.S. Department of State, 2023 Country Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107615.html); [Freedom House, Freedom on the Net 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/vietnam/freedom-net/2023); [Associated Press on Hồng’s conviction](https://apnews.com/article/5f18dcbcbc839de4c479c475b8b552f6); [Amnesty International, Vietnam 2023](https://www.vietnamhumanrights.net/english/documents/AI_VietNam2023.pdf).

---

### Record 5 — Strategic diplomatic balancing and the U.S. partnership

**Fact.** On 10 September 2023, Trọng hosted U.S. President Joe Biden in Hanoi and presided over the elevation of Vietnam-U.S. relations to a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership. The agreement expanded cooperation in technology, supply chains, education, climate, health, security, and semiconductors.

**Why it matters.** This was a major diplomatic achievement and an example of Trọng’s “bamboo diplomacy”: maintaining strategic autonomy while deepening relations with competing major powers. Vietnam nevertheless retained close defense and political ties with Russia and avoided aligning fully against China or Moscow.

**Strongest source.** “Joint Leaders’ Statement on elevating Viet Nam-U.S. ties to comprehensive strategic partnership,” Government of Vietnam, 11 September 2023. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/joint-leaders-statement-on-elevating-viet-nam-us-ties-to-comprehensive-strategic-partnership-111230911175640158.htm)

**Locator.** Statement of the September 10–11 visit and the elevation of bilateral relations; sections on economic, technology, climate, security, and educational cooperation.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Attribution strength.** High: the meeting was initiated at Trọng’s invitation and formally conducted between him and Biden.

**Complicating evidence.** The partnership was also driven by mutual strategic and economic interests, concern about China, and broader state-to-state preparation; it cannot be credited to Trọng alone.

**Cautions.** Official diplomatic statements describe intentions and commitments, not necessarily completed outcomes.

**Independent support.** [American Presidency Project fact sheet](https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/fact-sheet-president-joseph-r-biden-and-general-secretary-nguyen-phu-trong-announce-the-us); [Associated Press analysis](https://apnews.com/article/ac8f9dd899f77910c42295769d3fedb6).

---

### Record 6 — Security, military posture, and the Ukraine/Russia dilemma

**Fact.** Vietnam maintained a cautious, non-aligned position toward Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, avoiding direct condemnation of Moscow in key UN votes while preserving its longstanding defense relationship with Russia. At the same time, Vietnam strengthened security and diplomatic ties with the United States and emphasized sovereignty, strategic autonomy, and opposition to coercion.

**Why it matters.** The policy reduced the risk of entering a bloc confrontation and preserved defense options, but limited Vietnam’s alignment with Western sanctions and exposed tensions between its stated support for international law and its treatment of Russia.

**Strongest source.** Vietnam’s official 2023 foreign-policy speech by Trọng at the 32nd Diplomatic Conference. [Direct link](https://baochinhphu.vn/toan-van-phat-bieu-cua-tong-bi-thu-nguyen-phu-trong-tai-hoi-nghi-ngoai-giao-lan-thu-32-102231219155116287.htm)

**Locator.** Sections outlining Vietnam’s independent, self-reliant, multilateral foreign policy and strategic balancing.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023; the Russia/Ukraine position was inherited from 2022.

**Attribution strength.** High for the doctrine; moderate for individual diplomatic votes and military decisions, which involved the Foreign Ministry, government, and Party leadership collectively.

**Complicating evidence.** Vietnam’s government presented the policy as principled neutrality and non-alignment rather than support for Russia.

**Cautions.** Official doctrine is not sufficient to establish the motives behind particular votes or arms decisions.

**Independent support.** [Associated Press, Biden’s Vietnam visit](https://apnews.com/article/ac8f9dd899e7d93d89a2e4f321cf3664); [SIPRI military-expenditure data supplied in the local briefing]; [archived 2019 Vietnam Defence White Paper](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3A2019_Vietnam_National_Defence_White_Paper.pdf).

---

### Record 7 — Public safety and the Hanoi apartment fire

**Fact.** A fire in a nine-story apartment building in Hanoi in September 2023 killed at least 56 people and injured at least 37. The building was difficult for firefighters to access, lacked an emergency exit, and was located in a narrow alley. Authorities detained the owner and promised stronger fire-safety enforcement.

**Why it matters.** The incident exposed weaknesses in urban construction control, fire-code compliance, emergency access, and local enforcement. It is a government-performance issue, but attribution to Trọng personally is weak.

**Strongest source.** “Fire in Vietnam’s capital kills at least 56,” Associated Press, 13 September 2023. [Direct link](https://apnews.com/article/8db1a0259a5bfe4ccae7422e6aaa34f1)

**Locator.** Description of the death toll, building conditions, rescue difficulties, investigation, and detention of the owner.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Attribution strength.** Low to moderate for Trọng; higher for municipal and national regulatory institutions.

**Complicating evidence.** The owner’s alleged violations and local enforcement failures may explain more of the incident than central Party policy.

**Cautions.** Initial casualty figures changed as hospitals and authorities reconciled records.

**Independent support.** [Vietnam Fire and Rescue Police 2023 report](https://cspccc.canhsatpccc.gov.vn/vi/news/thong-cao-bao-chi/thong-cao-bao-chi-ve-pccc-va-cnch-nam-2023-3827); [Vietnam Ministry of Public Security annual safety report](https://mps.gov.vn/tin-tuc/tinh-hinh-tai-nan-giao-thong-chay-no-va-xu-ly-hanh-chinh-ve-trat-tu-an-toan-giao-thong-nam-2023-t37769.html).

---

### Record 8 — Social services and post-pandemic recovery

**Fact.** The government’s 2023 program emphasized restoring normal social and economic activity after COVID-19, expanding health-insurance coverage, reducing poverty, improving infrastructure, and protecting vulnerable groups. Official reporting stated that COVID-19 was largely controlled and economic activity had normalized.

**Why it matters.** Vietnam’s recovery and broad service provision were important achievements, but national averages do not establish equal access or service quality across regions and groups.

**Strongest source.** Trọng’s May 2023 midterm Central Committee speech. [Direct link](https://baochinhphu.vn/phat-bieu-cua-tong-bi-thu-nguyen-phu-trong-be-mac-hoi-nghi-trung-uong-giua-nhiem-ky-10223051717175679.htm)

**Locator.** Sections reporting COVID-19 control, economic recovery, macroeconomic stability, and social normalization.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023 and the preceding recovery period.

**Attribution strength.** Moderate: the program was centrally endorsed, but delivery depended on ministries, local government, health institutions, and budget execution.

**Complicating evidence.** The World Bank identified weak domestic demand, external shocks, and implementation constraints; health and poverty outcomes varied substantially by locality and population group.

**Cautions.** Official speeches are advocacy and self-assessment documents. They should be paired with household surveys, health-system studies, and distributional data.

**Independent support.** [WHO primary-health-care case study](https://ahpsr.who.int/publications/i/item/viet-nam-a-primary-health-care-case-study-in-the-context-of-the-covid-19-pandemic); [World Bank Vietnam Poverty and Equity Assessment](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099115004242216918); [World Bank 2023 economic update](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/08/10/vietnam-s-economic-growth-slows-due-to-global-headwinds-and-internal-constraints).

## 3. Later retrospective evidence

These sources were opened or inspected for context but should not be treated as direct 2023 evidence unless they explicitly report a 2023 event.

- **Trọng’s role in the anti-corruption campaign:** [Le Monde retrospective](https://www.lemonde.fr/en/obituaries/article/2024/07/20/nguyen-phu-trong-symbol-of-vietnamese-authoritarianism-dies-in-hanoi_6691391_15.html); [CSIS retrospective](https://www.csis.org/analysis/passing-vietnam-party-chief-nguyen-phu-trong).
- **Van Thinh Phat corruption case:** [Associated Press](https://apnews.com/article/943b7ab86e88905ebc7334c13cf123ff); [Associated Press trial report](https://apnews.com/article/a1bd2395ec22b718f39ba4a10cff969f).
- **Continuation of repression after Trọng:** [Associated Press on later leadership consolidation](https://apnews.com/article/5bdceb29e3f95bf612b04f35fc329e8f); useful for institutional legacy, not for scoring 2023 outcomes.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Vietnam is a one-party state without competitive national elections capable of replacing the CPV. Independent media, political opposition, labor organizing, and many civil-society activities are restricted. Online speech is regulated through the Cybersecurity Law and related decrees. Courts lack the level of independence needed to treat convictions in politically sensitive cases as neutral evidence without examining the underlying proceedings.

Reliable information is easier to obtain for:

- official appointments, laws, budgets, diplomatic statements, and macroeconomic statistics;
- major disasters and publicly acknowledged investigations;
- formal Party speeches and resolutions.

It is harder to obtain for:

- internal factional bargaining and Trọng’s personal role in personnel decisions;
- coercive policing, torture, detention conditions, and informal surveillance;
- corruption networks involving senior officials;
- unreported protests, local land disputes, and victims’ accounts;
- whether official targets were implemented in poorer provinces or among minorities;
- the motives behind Russia-related diplomacy and selective prosecutions.

The principal safeguards are triangulation, separating official claims from independently verified facts, recording source limitations, and distinguishing system-level conditions from decisions directly attributable to Trọng.

## 5. Questions for deeper research

1. Which 2023 personnel decisions can be documented as personally directed by Trọng rather than collectively approved by the Politburo or Central Committee?
2. Did the anti-corruption campaign measurably improve public-sector integrity, or mainly increase elite turnover and administrative risk aversion?
3. What were the actual outcomes of the September 2023 U.S. partnership commitments by the end of the year?
4. How did Vietnam’s Russia policy reconcile “independent” diplomacy with abstentions on Ukraine-related UN resolutions?
5. What regional and socioeconomic groups experienced the greatest economic or health-service setbacks in 2023?
6. How many political prisoners, journalists, environmental defenders, and labor organizers were detained or prosecuted during the calendar year?
7. What internal records explain the resignation of President Nguyễn Xuân Phúc and other officials?
8. Did central fire-safety directives change enforcement after the Hanoi apartment fire?
9. What evidence exists of corruption or conflicts of interest involving Party, police, military, or provincial elites that did not reach prosecution?
10. How should the 2023 indicators be adjusted for censorship, missing data, and the inability to survey dissenting populations freely?

## 6. Corroborating sources opened and useful mainly for support

- [U.S. Department of State, 2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Vietnam](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107615.html)
- [Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2023: Vietnam](https://freedomhouse.org/country/vietnam/freedom-world/2023)
- [Freedom House, Freedom on the Net 2023: Vietnam](https://freedomhouse.org/country/vietnam/freedom-net/2023)
- [World Bank, Vietnam economic update](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/08/10/vietnam-s-economic-growth-slows-due-to-global-headwinds-and-internal-constraints)
- [Vietnam National Statistics Office, 2023 socioeconomic report](https://www.gso.gov.vn/en/data-and-statistics/2024/02/socio-economic-situation-in-the-fourth-quarter-and-2023/)
- [Vietnam Government, U.S.-Vietnam joint statement](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/joint-leaders-statement-on-elevating-viet-nam-us-ties-to-comprehensive-strategic-partnership-111230911175640158.htm)
- [Vietnam Government, 2023 socioeconomic plan](https://baochinhphu.vn/nghi-quyet-ve-ke-hoach-phat-trien-kinh-te-xa-hoi-nam-2023-102221130171714827.htm)
- [WHO primary-health-care case study](https://ahpsr.who.int/publications/i/item/viet-nam-a-primary-health-care-case-study-in-the-context-of-the-covid-19-pandemic)
- [Amnesty International, Vietnam 2023](https://www.vietnamhumanrights.net/english/documents/AI_VietNam2023.pdf)

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- The full 2023 U.S. State Department country report at the official State Department archive.
- Full-text Vietnamese Party Central Committee personnel and anti-corruption decisions.
- The complete PAPI 2023 report, including provincial-level corruption and public-service findings.
- The full 2023 Vietnam Fire and Rescue Police statistical release and Hanoi inspection records.
- UN treaty-body materials concerning Vietnam’s ICCPR and anti-torture obligations.
- Detailed 2023 household, poverty, health-access, education, and regional inequality datasets.
- Full text of the 2023 Law on Housing and implementation records.
- Vietnamese and international reporting on the Việt Á, rescue-flight, and Van Thinh Phat cases, separating events occurring in 2023 from later trials.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Chapter 1B — Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility  
## Nguyễn Phú Trọng, Vietnam, 2023

No score assigned.

## 1. Orientation

Nguyễn Phú Trọng was General Secretary of the Communist Party of Vietnam during 2023 and the country’s most influential political actor. His authority operated through the Politburo, Central Committee, government, Ministry of Science and Technology, Ministry of National Defence, Ministry of Public Security, diplomatic service, and National Assembly. Nuclear policy was not a personal presidential portfolio. Attribution is therefore strongest where Trọng set strategic doctrine, chaired Party leadership, issued foreign-policy statements, or endorsed major national priorities.

Vietnam had no nuclear weapons and no operating commercial nuclear power plants in 2023. Its relevant exposure was instead:

- safeguards and nonproliferation;
- the Dalat 500 kW research reactor;
- plans for a future 10–15 MW research reactor;
- radioactive-material security and emergency preparedness;
- cyber, artificial-intelligence, biological, and other dual-use risks;
- strategic tensions involving China, Russia, the United States, and the South China Sea.

The inherited posture was broadly risk-reducing: Vietnam was party to the NPT safeguards system, the CTBT, the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons, the Southeast Asian Nuclear-Weapon-Free Zone framework, and IAEA nuclear-safety and physical-protection conventions. The main unresolved risks were institutional capacity, aging research-reactor infrastructure, incomplete development of future nuclear infrastructure, limited transparency, and the possibility that defense, cyber, or AI policy would prioritize state control over independent safety review.

The evidence base is stronger for treaty status, official doctrine, reactor infrastructure, and formal diplomatic statements than for budgets, operational safeguards, expert dissent, or Trọng’s personal role in technical decisions. Vietnam’s official reports are useful but self-evaluative. Independent specialist assessments are relatively limited, and several supplied sources were inaccessible through the research interface.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 1B.1 Reducing existential risk | Partially evidenced | WEB-1B-001, WEB-1B-003, WEB-1B-005 |
| 1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric | Partially evidenced | WEB-1B-004, WEB-1B-005 |
| 1B.3 Safe nuclear control | Partially evidenced; important gaps | WEB-1B-002, WEB-1B-003 |
| 1B.4 Arms control and inspections | Evidenced at formal level; implementation incomplete | WEB-1B-001, WEB-1B-003 |
| 1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression | No evidence of nuclear coercion; conventional strategic evidence is limited | WEB-1B-004, WEB-1B-005 |
| 1B.6 Preventing proliferation | Evidenced at treaty and safeguards level; enforcement detail limited | WEB-1B-001, WEB-1B-003 |
| 1B.7 Independent risk expertise | Partial evidence only | WEB-1B-002, WEB-1B-006 |
| 1B.8 Crisis de-escalation | Partial evidence through strategic-autonomy diplomacy; no nuclear crisis identified | WEB-1B-004, WEB-1B-005 |
| 1B.9 Other catastrophic risks | Formal cyber and AI strategies existed, but safeguards and independent review remain insufficiently documented | WEB-1B-007, WEB-1B-008 |
| 1B.10 Lasting risk posture | Unresolved; inherited safeguards continued, but durability and implementation require deeper research | WEB-1B-001, WEB-1B-002, WEB-1B-003 |

## 3. Evidence index

### WEB-1B-001 — Vietnam’s treaty and safeguards posture

**Claim.** Vietnam maintained a formal nonproliferation and nuclear-safety posture in 2023. It had an NPT safeguards agreement in force, an Additional Protocol signed in 2007 and in force from 2012, CTBT ratification, and participation in major nuclear-safety, radioactive-waste, nuclear-accident-assistance, and physical-protection conventions.

**Why it matters.** These commitments reduced the legal and institutional risk of diversion of nuclear material and prohibited nuclear testing. They were inherited conditions rather than a new 2023 Trọng initiative, so they should not be treated as a personal achievement without evidence of active 2023 maintenance or enforcement.

**Strongest source.** *Viet Nam, Socialist Republic of — Country Fact Sheet*, IAEA Office of Legal Affairs, updated 2026 but reporting treaty status. [Direct link](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=VN)

**Locator.** “Agreements” and “Safeguards Agreements” tables: CPPNM and its amendment, Early Notification Convention, Assistance Convention, Convention on Nuclear Safety, Joint Convention, NPT safeguards agreement, and Additional Protocol.

**Period fit.** Inherited and continuing during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. Treaty status was state policy, not demonstrably Trọng’s personal decision in 2023. His influence is indirect through Party-state strategic direction.

**Source confidence.** High for treaty status; lower for actual implementation.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Vietnam was not party to the Vienna Convention on Civil Liability for Nuclear Damage or the Convention on Supplementary Compensation. Formal treaty participation therefore did not eliminate all liability or compensation gaps.

**Independent corroboration.** [CTBTO record of Vietnam’s 2006 CTBT ratification](https://www.ctbto.org/resources/for-the-media/press-releases/viet-nam-becomes-thirty-fourth-annex-2-state-ratify); [ICAN Vietnam profile](https://www.icanw.org/vietnam).

---

### WEB-1B-002 — No commercial nuclear installations, but an aging research reactor remained

**Claim.** Vietnam had no nuclear installation within the Convention on Nuclear Safety’s commercial-reactor scope in the relevant report, but operated the 500 kW Dalat Nuclear Research Reactor. The reactor had aging components, including a tank more than 55 years old and corrosion concerns in cooling-pipeline systems.

**Why it matters.** Vietnam’s catastrophic nuclear risk was much smaller than that of a nuclear-armed or commercial-reactor state, but safe operation still required competent maintenance, inspection, licensing, emergency preparedness, and independent regulatory oversight.

**Strongest source.** *The Socialist Republic of Vietnam: National Report on Compliance to the Convention on Nuclear Safety*, Ministry of Science and Technology, submitted for the Joint Eighth and Ninth Review Meeting in 2023. [Direct PDF](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/vietnam_cns_national_report_8-9th_rm_in_2023.pdf)

**Locator.** pp. 2–5, lines 32–119: national nuclear program, absence of commercial installations, planned research reactor, and institutional responsibilities. pp. 5–7, lines 129–217: Dalat reactor, aging systems, corrosion, control-rod incident, and corrective maintenance.

**Period fit.** The report was prepared in 2022 for the 2023 review process; it describes the posture inherited into 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low for individual technical conditions; moderate for national nuclear policy and resource priorities.

**Source confidence.** High for disclosed infrastructure and official safety narrative; moderate for completeness because it is a government self-assessment.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The report records corrective maintenance, periodic inspection, incident reporting, and regulatory oversight. It does not establish that the aging equipment created an unacceptable immediate risk.

**Independent corroboration.** [IAEA country fact sheet](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=VN); [IAEA nuclear-power infrastructure publication](https://www-pub.iaea.org/mtcd/publications/pdf/pub1301_web.pdf).

---

### WEB-1B-003 — Safeguards, HEU-to-LEU conversion, and physical protection

**Claim.** Vietnam completed conversion of the Dalat research reactor from highly enriched uranium to low-enriched uranium, repatriated the spent HEU fuel to Russia in 2013, and operated the facility under licensing and oversight by the Vietnam Agency for Radiation and Nuclear Safety (VARANS).

**Why it matters.** HEU removal and low-enriched fuel use materially reduced proliferation and nuclear-security risks. The controls were established before Trọng’s 2023 period, but they formed the baseline he inherited and continued to govern during 2023.

**Strongest source.** *The Socialist Republic of Vietnam: National Report on Compliance to the Convention on Nuclear Safety*, Ministry of Science and Technology, 2023 review submission. [Direct PDF](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/vietnam_cns_national_report_8-9th_rm_in_2023.pdf)

**Locator.** pp. 5–8, lines 141–178 and 222–239: original HEU fuel, conversion to 19.75% LEU, repatriation of HEU spent fuel, physical-protection upgrades, licensing, and VARANS oversight.

**Period fit.** Inherited baseline continuing into 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. This was a state technical program involving the Ministry of Science and Technology, VARANS, NRI, the IAEA, and Russia; no evidence links the conversion personally to Trọng.

**Source confidence.** High for the technical history; moderate for independent verification because the source is official.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The report acknowledges aging systems and the need for continued maintenance. HEU removal did not eliminate risks from radioactive sources, cyber intrusion, sabotage, or future reactor construction.

**Independent corroboration.** [IAEA safeguards country record](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=VN); [IAEA reactor-safety materials](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/vietnam_cns_national_report_8-9th_rm_in_2023.pdf).

---

### WEB-1B-004 — Strategic-autonomy diplomacy reduced escalation incentives

**Claim.** Trọng’s 2023 diplomatic rhetoric emphasized independence, self-reliance, multilateralism, strategic autonomy, and preservation of a peaceful environment. Vietnam deepened ties with the United States while retaining relations with China and Russia, including the September 2023 elevation of Vietnam-U.S. relations to a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership.

**Why it matters.** This posture was de-escalatory in the sense that Vietnam avoided formal alignment with a nuclear-armed bloc and sought room for maneuver among major powers. It also reduced incentives for Vietnam to pursue an indigenous nuclear deterrent. However, this is an inference about risk posture, not proof of a specific nuclear-risk intervention.

**Strongest source.** *Full Speech by General Secretary Nguyễn Phú Trọng at the 32nd Diplomatic Conference*, Government of Vietnam, 19 December 2023. [Direct link](https://baochinhphu.vn/toan-van-phat-bieu-cua-tong-bi-thu-nguyen-phu-trong-tai-hoi-nghi-ngoai-giao-lan-thu-32-102231219155116287.htm)

**Locator.** Sections describing independent, self-reliant, multilateral foreign policy and preservation of peace and stability.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** High for rhetoric and strategic political framing; moderate for operational diplomacy, which involved the Foreign Ministry and wider Party-state leadership.

**Source confidence.** High for what Trọng said; moderate for whether the rhetoric accurately described all operational decisions.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Vietnam maintained close defense relations with Russia and avoided full alignment with Western positions on Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. Strategic ambiguity can reduce immediate escalation but can also weaken clarity about crisis signaling.

**Independent corroboration.** [Associated Press reporting on Biden’s Vietnam visit](https://apnews.com/article/ac8f9dd899e7d93d89a2e4f321cf3664); [U.S. government fact sheet](https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/fact-sheet-president-joseph-r-biden-and-general-secretary-nguyen-phu-trong-announce-the-us).

---

### WEB-1B-005 — U.S.-Vietnam partnership and non-escalatory strategic engagement

**Claim.** On 10 September 2023, Trọng and President Joe Biden elevated bilateral relations to a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership. The agreement covered peace, security, economic resilience, technology, climate, health, education, and semiconductors.

**Why it matters.** The agreement expanded communication and cooperation with a major nuclear-armed power without creating a mutual-defense treaty or nuclear umbrella. It may have improved Vietnam’s ability to hedge against coercion, but it also occurred amid U.S.-China strategic competition and should not automatically be characterized as purely risk-reducing.

**Strongest source.** *Joint Leaders’ Statement on Elevating Viet Nam-U.S. Ties to Comprehensive Strategic Partnership*, Government of Vietnam, 11 September 2023. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/joint-leaders-statement-on-elevating-viet-nam-us-ties-to-comprehensive-strategic-partnership-111230911175640158.htm)

**Locator.** Statement of the September 10–11 visit and sections describing cooperation areas.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** High for the meeting and formal announcement; moderate for the broader partnership, which required government and diplomatic implementation.

**Source confidence.** High for the formal agreement; moderate for its practical effects.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The partnership reflected mutual strategic and economic interests, including concern about China. It did not establish Vietnamese nuclear guarantees or resolve regional military risks.

**Independent corroboration.** [American Presidency Project fact sheet](https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/fact-sheet-president-joseph-r-biden-and-general-secretary-nguyen-phu-trong-announce-the-us); [U.S. Government Publishing Office record](https://www.govinfo.gov/app/details/DCPD-202300775).

---

### WEB-1B-006 — Nuclear expertise was institutionally present but independence is unclear

**Claim.** Vietnam maintained specialized nuclear institutions, including the Ministry of Science and Technology, VARANS, the Vietnam Atomic Energy Institute, the Nuclear Research Institute, the National Nuclear Safety Council, and the National Atomic Energy Council. These bodies were responsible for safety regulation, technical research, licensing, and advice to the government.

**Why it matters.** The institutional presence of technical bodies is evidence against complete substitution of expertise by political loyalty. However, the available evidence does not establish that experts had protected dissent, budgetary independence, or authority to overrule political priorities.

**Strongest source.** *The Socialist Republic of Vietnam: National Report on Compliance to the Convention on Nuclear Safety*, Ministry of Science and Technology. [Direct PDF](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/vietnam_cns_national_report_8-9th_rm_in_2023.pdf)

**Locator.** pp. 2–4, lines 44–52 and 120–126; pp. 7–8, lines 222–227.

**Period fit.** Institutional baseline continuing during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low. These institutions predated 2023 and reported through ministerial and governmental structures.

**Source confidence.** High for formal institutional existence; low to moderate for independence and dissent protection.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Vietnam’s broader political system restricts independent civil society and media scrutiny. That does not prove technical regulators were ineffective, but it limits external verification.

**Independent corroboration.** [IAEA nuclear-safety publications](https://www.iaea.org/ja/publications/reports); [IAEA country fact sheet](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=VN).

---

### WEB-1B-007 — Cybersecurity strategy prioritized state control and critical infrastructure

**Claim.** Vietnam’s national cybersecurity strategy prioritized Party leadership, state management, sovereignty in cyberspace, protection of digital infrastructure and data, and security of sectors including transport, energy, health, finance, defense, and government administration.

**Why it matters.** Protecting critical infrastructure is relevant to catastrophic-risk reduction. However, the strategy also concentrated authority in the Party and state, creating a tension between infrastructure security and independent oversight, transparency, and protection of researchers or civil-society monitors who might identify vulnerabilities.

**Strongest source.** *Government issues national strategy to combat new cybersecurity challenges*, Vietnam Government News, 2022. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/govt-issues-national-strategy-to-combat-new-cyber-security-challenges-111220811103436282.htm)

**Locator.** Strategy priorities: Party leadership, state management, cyberspace sovereignty, critical infrastructure, and the 11 protected sectors.

**Period fit.** Inherited strategy implemented during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. The strategy was a government policy rather than a documented personal Trọng directive.

**Source confidence.** High for formal policy content; moderate for implementation.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The same cybersecurity framework has been criticized by rights organizations for restricting online speech and independent scrutiny. Security and political control were pursued together.

**Independent corroboration.** [Freedom House, Freedom on the Net 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/vietnam/freedom-net/2023); [Vietnam Ministry of Science and Technology legal inventory for 2023](https://mst.gov.vn/cac-van-ban-quy-pham-phap-luat-ve-an-ninh-an-toan-thong-tin-duoc-ban-hanh-tai-viet-nam-nam-2023-197240815104459033.htm).

---

### WEB-1B-008 — AI development was promoted without a documented 2023 catastrophic-risk framework

**Claim.** Vietnam’s national AI strategy, adopted in 2021, promoted AI research, national data and high-performance-computing centers, defense and security applications, and domestic AI enterprises. The available 2023 evidence does not show a dedicated, independently reviewed framework for frontier-AI catastrophic risks, model evaluations, compute governance, or biological and cyber dual-use controls.

**Why it matters.** The policy created capacity and state interest in AI but emphasized development and national competitiveness. Absence of evidence is not evidence that safeguards did not exist; it identifies a material research gap.

**Strongest source.** *National Strategy on Research, Development and Application of Artificial Intelligence*, Government of Vietnam. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/national-strategy-on-rd-and-application-of-artificial-intelligence-11140663.htm)

**Locator.** Strategy sections on AI research, national data centers, high-performance computing, defense and security applications, and enterprise development.

**Period fit.** Inherited policy in force during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low. The strategy was issued under the Prime Minister in 2021; no evidence links its content specifically to Trọng.

**Source confidence.** High for formal strategy content; low for the negative finding that no further safeguards existed.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Vietnam may have used general cybersecurity, data-protection, defense, biosafety, or export-control rules that were not captured in the inspected strategy. These require separate inspection.

**Independent corroboration.** [Decision 127/QD-TTg legal record](https://congbao.chinhphu.vn/van-ban/quyet-dinh-so-127-qd-ttg-33096/34502.htm); [UNESCO AI readiness assessment](https://articles.unesco.org/sites/default/files/medias/fichiers/2025/10/Viet%20Nam%20Artificial%20Intelligence%20Readiness%20Assessment%20Report.pdf).

---

## 4. Overall factual assessment

The evidence does not show Vietnam possessing nuclear weapons, threatening nuclear use, using nuclear capability to shield aggression, or abandoning nonproliferation commitments in 2023. The country’s inherited nuclear posture was comparatively low-risk: no nuclear arsenal, IAEA safeguards, CTBT ratification, physical-protection commitments, and prior removal of HEU from the Dalat research reactor.

The strongest positive evidence concerns continuity of safeguards, HEU removal, treaty participation, and Trọng’s preference for strategic autonomy and diplomatic balancing. The strongest unresolved risks concern aging research-reactor infrastructure, incomplete future-reactor planning, limited transparency around budgets and inspections, uncertain regulator independence, and the absence of clear evidence on cyber, AI, biological, and other dual-use safeguards.

Trọng’s personal responsibility is therefore clearest for high-level diplomatic and strategic rhetoric, and weaker for technical nuclear administration. The available evidence supports a cautious conclusion that Vietnam’s nuclear posture remained broadly nonproliferation-oriented during 2023, while leaving substantial uncertainty about institutional durability and non-nuclear catastrophic risks.

## 5. Information limitations

- Vietnam’s official nuclear reports are self-evaluations and may omit failures, dissent, or resource shortages.
- Public budgets for VARANS, nuclear security, research-reactor maintenance, and cyber/AI safeguards were not located.
- The research interface blocked several IAEA conference papers, UN statements, NTI materials, and PDF candidates.
- No public record was found showing Trọng personally intervened in reactor licensing, safeguards, nuclear-security budgets, or AI-risk policy.
- Treaty participation establishes formal commitments, not complete compliance.
- The absence of nuclear threats or accidents cannot prove that command, custody, or emergency systems were fully safe.
- Vietnam’s censorship and restricted civil society make independent confirmation of sensitive security failures difficult.

## 6. Priority questions for deeper research

1. Did Vietnam receive an IAEA safeguards conclusion for 2023, and were there any unresolved questions or complementary-access limitations?
2. Was the Dalat reactor operating license renewed in February 2023, and what conditions or corrective actions accompanied renewal?
3. What findings came from the IAEA International Nuclear Security Advisory Mission reportedly conducted in March 2023?
4. What resources were allocated to VARANS, nuclear security, emergency preparedness, and research-reactor modernization?
5. Did Vietnam’s 2023 diplomatic statements explicitly address nuclear disarmament, no-first-use, negative security assurances, or nuclear risk reduction?
6. What was Vietnam’s position at the 2023 NPT-related meetings, CTBT activities, ASEAN nuclear-weapon-free-zone mechanisms, and IAEA General Conference?
7. What controls governed radioactive sources, cyber systems at nuclear facilities, and insider threats?
8. Did the government’s cyber and AI strategies include independent audits, incident reporting, safety testing, or protections for expert dissent?
9. What biological-security and dual-use research controls were operating in 2023?
10. Did the 2023 U.S. partnership create new military, cyber, semiconductor, or intelligence risks relevant to escalation with China?

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# Chapter 2B — International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security  
## Nguyễn Phú Trọng, Vietnam, 2023

No score assigned.

## 1. Orientation and conflict-exposure gate

Vietnam was not engaged in an interstate war in 2023 and did not initiate a cross-border war, annexation campaign, proxy war, or overt military intervention. The main international-security exposures were:

- China–Vietnam maritime and territorial disputes in the South China Sea;
- strategic competition between China and the United States;
- Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and Vietnam’s longstanding defense relationship with Russia;
- modernization of Vietnam’s armed forces;
- border and maritime security;
- participation in UN peacekeeping and humanitarian operations.

Nguyễn Phú Trọng, as CPV General Secretary, had major influence over strategic diplomacy, defense doctrine, and Party direction. Operational authority remained distributed among the Politburo, Central Military Commission, Ministry of National Defence, Ministry of Foreign Affairs, government, and military command. Vietnam’s stated defense doctrine emphasized strategic autonomy, no military alliances, no foreign bases, no alignment against another country, and no use or threat of force in international relations.

The 2023 record is strongest for formal doctrine, diplomacy, public statements, and peacekeeping. It is weaker for classified military procurement, internal deliberation, maritime incidents, rules of engagement, and the precise role of Trọng in operational decisions.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 2B.1 Peaceful alternatives | Evidenced | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-002, WEB-2B-003 |
| 2B.2 Starting or prolonging war | No evidence of Vietnamese initiation or prolongation of interstate war | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-005 |
| 2B.3 Honest justification for force | Partially evidenced; rhetoric is reviewable but official claims are self-serving | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-004 |
| 2B.4 Civilian and prisoner protection | No active Vietnamese interstate war identified; humanitarian conduct documented | WEB-2B-006 |
| 2B.5 Control of proxies and arms | No Vietnamese proxy war identified; arms-transfer transparency remains a gap | WEB-2B-005 |
| 2B.6 Scrutiny of war claims | Limited; official security information is controlled and independent scrutiny is restricted | WEB-2B-007 |
| 2B.7 Ceasefires and settlements | Evidenced through diplomacy and peaceful dispute-management commitments; no major 2023 peace settlement identified | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-002 |
| 2B.8 Responsible military resources | Partially evidenced; SIPRI expenditure data available, procurement transparency limited | WEB-2B-005 |
| 2B.9 Accountability and remedy | No major 2023 international-conflict accountability event found; domestic transparency and judicial independence remain limitations | WEB-2B-007 |
| 2B.10 Lasting international peace | Partially evidenced; diplomacy broadened relationships and preserved strategic autonomy, but maritime and great-power risks remained unresolved | WEB-2B-001, WEB-2B-002, WEB-2B-003 |

## 3. Compact evidence index

### WEB-2B-001 — Strategic autonomy and peaceful settlement as governing doctrine

**Claim.** In his December 2023 speech to the 32nd Diplomatic Conference, Trọng described Vietnamese foreign policy as independent, self-reliant, peaceful, multilateral, flexible in tactics, and based on the UN Charter and international law. He said Vietnam sought to prevent war and conflict “early and from afar,” promote dialogue and negotiation, and settle territorial issues through respect for international law.

**Why it matters.** This is the clearest direct evidence of Trọng’s preferred international-security approach: deterrence and sovereignty protection combined with diplomatic restraint and avoidance of military alignment.

**Strongest source.** “Full speech by General Secretary Nguyễn Phú Trọng at the 32nd Diplomatic Conference,” Government of Vietnam, 19 December 2023. [Direct link](https://baochinhphu.vn/toan-van-phat-bieu-cua-tong-bi-thu-nguyen-phu-trong-tai-hoi-nghi-ngoai-giao-lan-thu-32-102231219155116287.htm)

**Locator.** Paragraphs describing the “Vietnamese bamboo diplomacy” doctrine; sections stating that Vietnam used dialogue, negotiation, international law, and early prevention of war and conflict.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** High for rhetoric and Party-level strategic direction; moderate for implementation by the Foreign Ministry, defense institutions, and government.

**Source confidence.** High for what Trọng said; moderate for the accuracy of official claims about outcomes.

**Contrary evidence.** The doctrine combines peace language with firm sovereignty defense, military modernization, and security cooperation. It does not establish that all coercive maritime conduct was avoided.

**Independent corroboration.** [Vietnam’s 2019 Defense White Paper summary](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/four-nos-principle-of-national-defense-policy-11137244.htm); [ISEAS analysis of Vietnam’s bamboo diplomacy](https://sealionplus.iseas.edu.sg/assets/displaypdf/34097).

---

### WEB-2B-002 — 2023 expansion of diplomatic ties with the United States

**Claim.** On 10 September 2023, Trọng and President Joe Biden elevated Vietnam–U.S. relations to a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership. The agreement covered peace, security, economic resilience, technology, education, climate, health, and supply-chain cooperation.

**Why it matters.** Vietnam strengthened relations with a major power without entering a military alliance or accepting foreign bases. This broadened diplomatic options and may have reduced dependence on any single partner, while also placing Vietnam more visibly inside U.S.–China strategic competition.

**Strongest source.** “Joint Leaders’ Statement on Elevating Viet Nam–U.S. Ties to Comprehensive Strategic Partnership,” Government of Vietnam, 11 September 2023. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/joint-leaders-statement-on-elevating-viet-nam-us-ties-to-comprehensive-strategic-partnership-111230911175640158.htm)

**Locator.** Opening paragraphs on the September 10–11 visit and sections listing cooperation in security, peace, technology, climate, health, and economic development.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** High for the meeting, invitation, and formal announcement; moderate for subsequent implementation.

**Source confidence.** High for the formal agreement; moderate for its longer-term security consequences.

**Contrary evidence.** The agreement did not create a mutual-defense commitment. It also reflected U.S. strategic interest in regional stability and China-related competition, not only Trọng’s initiative.

**Independent corroboration.** [American Presidency Project fact sheet](https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/fact-sheet-president-joseph-r-biden-and-general-secretary-nguyen-phu-trong-announce-the-us); [U.S. Government Publishing Office record](https://www.govinfo.gov/app/details/DCPD-202300775).

---

### WEB-2B-003 — China–Vietnam summit and continued risk-management

**Claim.** Vietnam and China issued a joint statement in December 2023 after Xi Jinping’s state visit. The two governments reaffirmed political ties, cooperation, and management of disagreements while maintaining the broader relationship.

**Why it matters.** Maintaining direct Party and state communication with China was a peaceful alternative to escalation amid maritime disputes and wider U.S.–China rivalry. It also shows the limits of interpreting Vietnam’s U.S. partnership as alignment against China.

**Strongest source.** “Viet Nam, China Issue Joint Statement,” Government of Vietnam, December 2023. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/viet-nam-china-joint-statement-111231213225010714.htm)

**Locator.** Sections on political trust, strategic cooperation, maritime issues, peaceful management of disputes, and continued bilateral communication.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** High for Party-level political direction; moderate for negotiation details handled by the government and Foreign Ministry.

**Source confidence.** High for the existence and wording of the formal statement; moderate for whether it reduced actual maritime coercion.

**Contrary evidence.** Bilateral statements are diplomatic instruments and may conceal unresolved disagreement. Vietnam’s maritime claims and China’s activities remained contested.

**Independent corroboration.** [Vietnam Foreign Ministry South China Sea statement](https://mofa.gov.vn/en/tt_baochi/pbnfn/ns230717101342); [2019 Vietnam Defense White Paper](https://mod.gov.vn/wcm/connect/08963129-c9cf-4c86-9b5c-81a9e2b14455/2019vietnamnationaldefence.pdf?mod=ajperes).

---

### WEB-2B-004 — “Four Nos” defense doctrine

**Claim.** Vietnam’s defense doctrine rejected military alliances, siding with one country against another, foreign military bases, and the use or threat of force in international relations. The policy also left room for defense cooperation and military relations short of formal alignment.

**Why it matters.** The doctrine constrained escalation and alliance entrapment while preserving autonomy. Its fourth principle directly addressed the use of force, although the doctrine’s practical meaning depended on how Vietnam defined self-defense and sovereignty protection.

**Strongest source.** “Four No’s principle of national defense policy,” Vietnam Government News, Ministry of National Defence statement. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/four-nos-principle-of-national-defense-policy-11137244.htm)

**Locator.** Passage defining the four principles and explaining that Vietnam did not join alliances, host foreign bases, align against another state, or use or threaten force internationally.

**Period fit.** Inherited doctrine reaffirmed during the target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The policy was a state and defense doctrine, not solely Trọng’s personal policy, but it aligned with his Party-level strategic-autonomy rhetoric.

**Source confidence.** High for the formal doctrine; moderate for operational compliance.

**Contrary evidence.** Vietnam expanded defense cooperation with multiple partners and continued military modernization. “No alliances” does not mean no strategic balancing or coercive capability.

**Independent corroboration.** [2023 Prime Ministerial reaffirmation of the Four Nos](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/prime-minister-reassures-viet-nams-four-nos-defense-policy-11123080610460922.htm); [2019 Defense White Paper summary](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/white-paper-on-national-defence-released-11136749.htm).

---

### WEB-2B-005 — Military expenditure and defensive modernization

**Claim.** SIPRI’s military-expenditure data recorded Vietnam’s 2023 military spending at approximately US$8.1 billion in constant 2022 dollars, or roughly US$79.5 per capita according to the local structured package. Vietnam continued defense modernization under a policy framed as national defense and sovereignty protection rather than expeditionary warfare.

**Why it matters.** Military resources can support deterrence and maritime defense, but can also intensify regional security dilemmas. The available public record does not establish that 2023 procurement was disproportionate, corrupt, or intended for aggression.

**Strongest source.** *SIPRI Military Expenditure Database* and *Trends in World Military Expenditure, 2023*, Stockholm International Peace Research Institute, 2024. [Database](https://www.sipri.org/databases/milex) · [2023 fact sheet](https://www.sipri.org/publications/2024/sipri-fact-sheets/trends-world-military-expenditure-2023)

**Locator.** Vietnam country series in the SIPRI database; the local structured record identifies 2023 expenditure of approximately US$8.116 billion in constant dollars.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for strategic defense policy; low for individual procurement and budget execution.

**Source confidence.** High for SIPRI’s standardized estimate; moderate for Vietnam-specific precision because military budgets are not fully transparent.

**Contrary evidence.** Official Vietnamese doctrine describes the military as defensive and rejects alliances and use of force. Conversely, modernization can increase regional uncertainty, particularly in the South China Sea.

**Independent corroboration.** [Vietnam 2019 Defense White Paper](https://mod.gov.vn/wcm/connect/08963129-c9cf-4c86-9b5c-81a9e2b14455/2019vietnamnationaldefence.pdf?mod=ajperes); [SIPRI Yearbook 2024 summary](https://www.sipri.org/sites/default/files/2024-06/yb24_summary_en_2_0.pdf).

---

### WEB-2B-006 — Humanitarian military deployment to Türkiye

**Claim.** In February 2023 Vietnam deployed military personnel, medical staff, rescuers, and search-and-rescue dog handlers to Türkiye after the earthquake. The government reported 76 soldiers in the initial deployment and later described the mission as including equipment and humanitarian assistance.

**Why it matters.** This was an international use of military capacity for humanitarian rescue rather than coercion or combat. It provides concrete evidence that Vietnamese military resources were used in a nonviolent, internationally cooperative way.

**Strongest source.** “Viet Nam sends soldiers to Turkey for quake rescue mission,” Vietnam Government News, 13 February 2023. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/viet-nam-sends-soldiers-to-turkey-for-quake-rescue-mission-111230213095459857.htm)

**Locator.** Report of the Ministry of National Defence deployment of 76 soldiers, including military doctors, rescuers, and dog trainers.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. The deployment was a government and Ministry of National Defence decision; no evidence shows Trọng personally ordered it.

**Source confidence.** Moderate to high for the deployment; official reporting may emphasize success and omit operational difficulties.

**Contrary evidence.** Humanitarian deployment does not establish how Vietnamese forces behaved in domestic security operations or classified military activities.

**Independent corroboration.** [Vietnam Government report on mission completion](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/vietnamese-army-rescue-team-completes-mission-in-turkey-111230222124252977.htm); [UN Vietnam peacekeeping profile](https://peacekeeping.un.org/en/vietnam).

---

### WEB-2B-007 — International security information was not subject to fully independent scrutiny

**Claim.** Vietnam’s formal security and foreign-policy accounts were produced within a tightly controlled one-party information environment. Independent media and civil-society groups faced restrictions, limiting external scrutiny of maritime incidents, defense procurement, military conduct, and official threat claims.

**Why it matters.** The absence of public evidence of unlawful force or proxy abuse cannot be treated as proof that such conduct did not occur. The reliability of official claims is stronger for formal treaties and statements than for casualty, procurement, or operational claims.

**Strongest source.** *Vietnam: Freedom in the World 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House. [Direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/vietnam/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator.** Sections on restrictions on media, political participation, civil society, online speech, and imprisonment of journalists and activists.

**Period fit.** Primarily 2022–2023 baseline; relevant to 2023 information conditions.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for the Party-state political environment; low for individual foreign-policy information decisions.

**Source confidence.** Moderate to high for documented legal and institutional restrictions; lower for claims relying on inaccessible victims or detainees.

**Contrary evidence.** Official Vietnamese sources provide substantial public information about diplomatic meetings, defense doctrine, and humanitarian deployments. The limitation is selectivity and lack of independent access, not total absence of information.

**Independent corroboration.** [U.S. Department of State 2023 human-rights report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107615.html); [Freedom House, Freedom on the Net 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/vietnam/freedom-net/2023).

---

### WEB-2B-008 — UN peacekeeping and international cooperation

**Claim.** Vietnam continued participation in UN peacekeeping and reported that nearly 800 military and public-security personnel had served in UN missions since 2014. In 2023, Vietnam presented this role as contributing to local safety, trust, and international stability.

**Why it matters.** UN peacekeeping is a lawful, multilateral security activity and provides evidence of international engagement short of war. It does not by itself prove that all Vietnamese security conduct was lawful or that personnel accountability was sufficient.

**Strongest source.** “Viet Nam active in UN peacekeeping mission,” Vietnam Government News, 25 December 2023. [Direct link](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/viet-nam-active-in-un-peacekeeping-mission-111231225103014007.htm)

**Locator.** Statement that Vietnam had deployed nearly 800 military and public-security officers since joining UN peacekeeping in 2014 and description of their duties.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns the end of 2023, with broader cumulative context.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. This was a multi-year state policy, indirectly connected to Party direction.

**Source confidence.** Moderate. The deployment history is official; independent evidence on conduct and accountability is limited.

**Contrary evidence.** The source is self-presentational and does not provide detailed independent evaluations of each mission.

**Independent corroboration.** [UN Vietnam peacekeeping profile](https://peacekeeping.un.org/en/vietnam); [Trọng’s December 2023 diplomatic speech](https://baochinhphu.vn/toan-van-phat-bieu-cua-tong-bi-thu-nguyen-phu-trong-tai-hoi-nghi-ngoai-giao-lan-thu-32-102231219155116287.htm).

## 4. Overall factual assessment

The 2023 evidence supports a conflict-exposure finding of “no active Vietnamese interstate war,” with meaningful but managed maritime and great-power security risks.

There is no inspected evidence that Trọng initiated or prolonged a war, authorized annexation, used proxy forces abroad, or supported unlawful cross-border aggression. Vietnam’s formal policy favored diplomacy, international law, strategic autonomy, and non-alignment. Trọng personally promoted that approach in his foreign-policy speeches and used high-level diplomacy with both the United States and China.

The main positive evidence is:

- explicit commitment to dialogue, negotiation, and international law;
- avoidance of formal military alliances and foreign bases;
- continued diplomatic engagement with China despite disputes;
- expanded relations with the United States without a mutual-defense treaty;
- participation in UN peacekeeping and humanitarian rescue;
- no identified 2023 Vietnamese war of choice.

The principal uncertainties are:

- opaque defense procurement and military budgeting;
- limited independent scrutiny of maritime incidents and security claims;
- unclear rules of engagement around contested waters;
- limited public evidence on arms transfers and foreign military cooperation;
- the tension between “no use of force” rhetoric and military modernization;
- unresolved South China Sea disputes and continuing dependence on strategic ambiguity.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

Reliable evidence is relatively strong for:

- official diplomatic statements;
- formal defense doctrine;
- treaty and partnership documents;
- humanitarian deployments;
- publicly reported military-spending estimates.

It is weaker for:

- classified military procurement;
- military and coast-guard rules of engagement;
- maritime incidents involving China;
- internal debates over Russia’s invasion of Ukraine;
- civilian or fishing-vessel casualties;
- independent audits of defense spending;
- judicial or legislative review of security claims.

Vietnamese official sources are valuable primary evidence for formal positions but are not independent assessments. Independent reporting can improve verification but often relies on anonymous officials or is limited by restricted access. No major 2023 Vietnamese interstate-war casualty record or international legal finding against Vietnamese forces was located in the inspected material.

## 6. Priority questions for deeper research

1. What specific South China Sea incidents occurred in 2023, and how did Vietnamese authorities respond?
2. Did Vietnam conduct any covert maritime coercion, military harassment, or proxy activity not publicly acknowledged?
3. What were Vietnam’s exact UN votes and explanations concerning Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in 2023?
4. What defense procurement and arms-transfer decisions were made in 2023, and were they independently audited?
5. Did Vietnam transfer weapons or military technology to foreign governments or non-state actors?
6. What rules governed coast-guard and naval encounters with Chinese vessels?
7. Were any Vietnamese personnel investigated or disciplined for unlawful conduct abroad or in international operations?
8. What concrete implementation followed the Vietnam–U.S. and Vietnam–China 2023 agreements?
9. How much of the 2023 diplomatic success was personally directed by Trọng versus the collective Party, government, and Foreign Ministry?
10. Did defense spending improve genuine maritime deterrence or primarily reinforce security institutions and political control?

## 7. Source groups

- [Vietnam–U.S. Comprehensive Strategic Partnership statement](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/joint-leaders-statement-on-elevating-viet-nam-us-ties-to-comprehensive-strategic-partnership-111230911175640158.htm)
- [Vietnam–China joint statement](https://en.baochinhphu.vn/viet-nam-china-joint-statement-111231213225010714.htm)
